[n: Rom. xv, 1; Galat. vi, 2.] And a heavy burden, beyond any other among hard things, is the indifference of words about the same matters, which arises in the more childish through the shifting of times and persons, by deceit perhaps, or by threat, of those who coax, fawning or being exacting, and who fasten on unrestrainedly, above all in a time of the disorder of the whole. ⟦q19.1|48⟧ Stretching out a hand in sympathy, then, to your suppliant, may you not cease, now cleansing him by word with knowledge, now strengthening him by prayer with love for man, and making him kin to, and bringing him before, God the Word, rightly worshipped and believed by you, and King of all the ages, both now and at his fearful appearing; to whom with the Father and the Holy Spirit be honour, might, glory, worship, unto the ages of ages. ⟦q19.1|49⟧ Amen.

⟦q20.1|1+2+3+4+5⟧ OPUSCULUM 16 — ON THE TWO WILLS OF THE ONE CHRIST OUR GOD

⟦q20.1|1+2+3+4+5⟧ *Of Maximus the great, the confessor: On the two wills of the one Christ our God.*

⟦q20.1|1+2+3+4+5⟧ Different shapes and forms have been devised by art by different men, for safety and adornment together; and by these they think to make a fine show in what appears, having grown weary, through folly, of what is hidden. ⟦q20.1|1+2+3+4+5⟧ But for thee, sanctified Father, who hast already transferred thy thought to the life without interval, virtue unartful, surpassing every form and shape, has sufficed for beauty and for guard; adorning, besides what is [0185] unseen, what is seen by all as well, so greatly as thou hast preferred to what is dissolving and to the world [worldly] a longing that stands firm and never has satiety. ⟦q20.1|1+2+3+4+5⟧ Or rather, showing thee, as is right, so far made akin to God and like him, as it has declared thee wholly estranged from matter and the things about it in relation, and unlike them. ⟦q20.1|1+2+3+4+5⟧ Hence too the being uncapturable by the things that act ill is thine. ⟦q20.1|1+2+3+4+5⟧ For he is not of a nature to suffer at their hands who by inclination has suffered the good things, and is wholly held fast by them, and has received the fulfilment of longing, the perfect victory in himself of what is longed for, and the cessation of movement, the ever-moving rest; according to which the death that long ago prevailed over nature vanishes, nature being no longer worsted by it through transgression. ⟦q20.1|1+2+3+4+5⟧ For thou no longer undergoest it again, because of the prevailing grace of the Spirit, by which thou art held fast through holding fast to it in prayers; and, accomplishing its paths with knowledge, thou showest in operation which are royal and divine for those who wish to walk straight on the unerring path of piety, and markest off the turnings-aside from these on either side; and for the one thou showest the threatened griefs, for the other the promised holy recompenses, so that by fear and longing thou mayest lead them up to the perfection that is in God.

⟦q20.1|6⟧ And admiring thy work, I fittingly praise thine understanding; or rather, struck by thy vigour in both, glorifying God who empowers thee and makes thee wise, and entreating thee to use the same methods toward those lacking in wits, and not to grow weary — for virtue has no weariness, being the accomplisher of all good things — until thou put into them the mind of Christ, [n: I Cor. ii, 16.] and train them to search out the depths of the Spirit, [n: ibid. 10.] whose very manifest things circumscribe all the wisdom and power of beings, and who reveals the wisdom spoken in a mystery to those who, like thee, have attached themselves to him and have judged nothing more precious than living with him.

⟦q20.1|7+8+9⟧ But since, possessing the virtue that is first of all and likewise last, as one that leads forward and as a seal shining round — I mean humility — thou hast deigned to inquire of thy servant and disciple, on account of the definition of natural will which the devout monk made, whether it is well or not, where he said that it «is a power appetitive of what is according to nature, and holding together all the idioms that belong essentially to the nature»: I suppose that it is well, and that this is one will and that of inclination another, and not out of tune with the teaching of the God-approved Fathers; even if it was thought by some not to be so, who [0188] insist that the Fathers define will as volition, and as the thing willed or what is willed.

⟦q20.1|7+8+9⟧ And who defines it so, and which of the Fathers, I would gladly ask them. ⟦q20.1|7+8+9⟧ For if so, what else is this but a circular demonstration, which does not distinguish the principle of each by itself, but sets down, against all likelihood, the same principle of each through the other, and puts it forward; which is what it is to define will by volition, and this again by that? ⟦q20.1|7+8+9⟧ For the things defined convert with their own definition. ⟦q20.1|10⟧ For that they call will volition too is plain. ⟦q20.1|11⟧ Yet they do not say this as a definition of will. ⟦q20.1|12⟧ For who can show it? ⟦q20.1|13⟧ Since then wish will be a definition of purpose, and movement in turn of motion, and manhood of man, according to those who say so. ⟦q20.1|14⟧ But whether they signify what is signified in this way or in that, they in no way harm its existence, whether what is indicated be a movement or an essence. ⟦q20.1|15⟧ For if this harmed it, the existence of all universals together with particulars would altogether collapse. ⟦q20.1|16⟧ For in all things we make use of different significations.

⟦q20.1|17⟧ And this too I say: that even if they call will volition, yet in no way do they call it the thing willed or what is willed. ⟦q20.1|18⟧ For how are aspiration and the object of aspiration the same? ⟦q20.1|19⟧ For if so, toward what will it be moved, being itself properly that very thing toward which it is moved, and not being in nature other than that? ⟦q20.1|20+21+22⟧ For it is a middle relation between the extremes, uniting them through itself, but not concurring with them in existence. ⟦q20.1|20+21+22⟧ And witness is the divine and great Gregory, who does not at all bring the thing willed and the thing begotten into identity with volition and generation, but through these, as natural relations, refers them back to the one begetting and willing. ⟦q20.1|20+21+22⟧ For if he did not even consent to make the reference of the thing willed or begotten to the volition and the generation — though volition is middle according to nature — «For it does not altogether follow, he says, how was he going to bring them into identity, and declare these to be one?»

⟦q20.1|20+21+22⟧ Besides, if they say that will is the thing willed, and we are by nature a thing willed and what is willed — for we are God's creature — then God has by nature no other will at all but us alone, coming to be and passing away, as we do; and before we were, he was bereft of all will. ⟦q20.1|23⟧ And the same will be concluded in our case too: a will proper to us and natural will not belong to us; only the thing willed from outside, being or not being, good or not such, will be wrapped around us. ⟦q20.1|23⟧ Moreover, the thing willed is something among the things outside. ⟦q20.1|24+25+26+27+28⟧ For it is called a thing willed and what is willed because of the one moved toward it by volition, and not because of itself. ⟦q20.1|24+25+26+27+28⟧ So that if he who has natural volition wills none of the things that are, there is for him no thing willed or what is willed. ⟦q20.1|24+25+26+27+28⟧ What account, then, have those who insist that the thing willed is will, or who declare that it is so called by the holy Fathers?

⟦q20.1|24+25+26+27+28⟧ [0189] But let it be said so, according to them; for our discourse is not about a difference of names, but about pious concepts. ⟦q20.1|24+25+26+27+28⟧ But let them answer this to us who fittingly ask: whether the divine will is other according to nature in relation to the human, and this other in relation to that, even if it be called and named in ten thousand ways. ⟦q20.1|24+25+26+27+28⟧ Let them teach this, and make firm our understanding, which is shaken over what is unknown; and let us not be much vexed at their impositions of names, but even contribute something of our own to them. ⟦q20.1|24+25+26+27+28⟧ For if it is not other and other according to essence, it is plain that it is one by nature and the same. ⟦q20.1|29⟧ And if this, the nature too is of necessity one and invariant. ⟦q20.1|30⟧ For where there is no natural otherness of wills, neither is there at all otherness of essence and nature. ⟦q20.1|31⟧ But if it is other and other by nature, they are altogether different; and if different, how must one not confess their number in quantity? ⟦q20.1|32⟧ For the denial of this works the utter destruction both of the difference itself and of the things that differ, as not being. ⟦q20.1|33⟧ For how is it possible both to be and not to be numbered, and for what cause? ⟦q20.1|34⟧ Or how are the wills, being according to nature, not numbered in the same way, if indeed the natures themselves would reasonably not cease from being numbered? ⟦q20.1|35+36⟧ For what is good in the case of those, since they are innate in the things and constitutive of them, ought to hold also for the things themselves; and either let them remove number from these too, as injurious according to them, or, as in no way harmful, let it be included in the case of those too, for the indication of the innate difference present in them, on account of which we use it even with the essences; or let them show how they do not altogether work the destruction or the confusion of the Saviour's natural will as man, by their refusal of number.

⟦q20.1|37⟧ And how is the Word incarnate perfect man, apart from natural will? ⟦q20.1|38⟧ For being deified by the union with God does not remove it from its true being according to essence, as neither the very nature of the flesh animated rationally and intellectually; just as the utmost and thoroughgoing commingling and union with fire does not remove iron from its own; but it undergoes the things of fire, since it has become fire by the union; yet it is heavy again according to nature, and cuts, because it has not suffered damage to its own nature, nor departed at all from natural operation; though it exists with the fire according to the same and one hypostasis, and does without interval both the things according to its own nature — I mean the cutting — and in turn the things according to the union, which is the burning. ⟦q20.1|39⟧ For the burning belongs to it, as the cutting also belongs to the fire, because of the utmost interpenetration and exchange of these into one another; and neither its nature — I mean the iron's — even though it is seen with fire, are we [0192] at all prevented from naming or numbering, nor its natural operation, even though it is [?] with burning, and has no interval toward it, but shows through and is known uniformly with it and in it. ⟦q20.1|39⟧ What reason, then, is there not to number what belongs naturally to the nature, that is, the will? ⟦q20.1|40⟧ And thereby to attest an imperfection in what is ours to God the Word made flesh, more than perfect and before perfection, although, by the good pleasure of the Father and the co-operation of the Holy Spirit, for our salvation, he shared equally in all the things of the nature, according to the union without interval, as indeed in the nature itself perfectly, save only sin; which was not even of the nature, but of an inclination, the nature having been moved contrary to its principle and its law: a deviation and a slip; [?]

⟦q20.1|40⟧ And to me the said monk seems fittingly to have attended to this above all, having distinguished the will of inclination from the natural. ⟦q20.1|41⟧ For the one, according to his definition, which is well made, is a power appetitive of what is according to nature. ⟦q20.1|42⟧ For every being whatever, and above all every rational one, naturally has appetite for what is according to nature, and, having received from God the power for this, has it according to essence, for its own constitution. ⟦q20.1|43⟧ For if it has not the appetite for what is according to nature, could it have it for what in no way is — whence and how — when that has no existence or movement at all? ⟦q20.1|44⟧ For only non-being is inert and without essence; and in no way is there any power of it at all among beings, or movement. ⟦q20.1|45⟧ But the other is self-chosen impulse, making turnings-aside to either side; and it is not distinguishing of nature, but properly of person and hypostasis.

⟦q20.1|46⟧ And having discerned this, the divided and man-worshipping Nestorius impiously taught a union of inclinations; so that he might confirm each hypostasis through the other, and the mere man might be preserved for him, having made the union with God the Word by a certain inclination and self-chosen movement, as he himself says; from which he also establishes the identity of will, or, to speak more properly, the multiplicity of will; inasmuch as, according to him, the union goes forward part by part at each movement of the will of inclination, from the imperfect to the complete, being borne along and advancing; and not from the ineffable conception itself, the Godhead of the Word having been united whole, perfectly, with all that is its own, to our nature whole and all that pertains to it, according to one and the same hypostasis.

⟦q20.1|47⟧ So then, that the said monk might set out precisely the completeness of the nature in God incarnate, and cast away what is opposed: the natural will he [0193] defined in him; the will of inclination he marked off, giving neither room to division, through its being at strife in this, nor entry to phantasy, through that natural will's not existing. ⟦q20.1|48⟧ But if someone perhaps insists that it is not possible for the two wills to be spoken of in his case otherwise than by opposition: if as different according to essence, I too accept it; but if as opposed, the account is false. ⟦q20.1|49⟧ For not whatever is different is altogether also opposed. ⟦q20.1|50⟧ For what is opposed is the work of an inclination moved, plainly, contrary to reason; but what is different, of a nature governed by reason; and the one is seditious of nature, the other manifestly constitutive. ⟦q20.1|51⟧ Essential difference, then, is for the constitution of the things in the nature; by no means opposition, which is for their dissolution. ⟦q20.1|52⟧ For nature does not have what is contrary to nature; nor is there any principle of what is at strife.

⟦q20.1|53⟧ Besides, if two wills are altogether contrary and opposed, how is it that in the case of rational beings divided by nature and hypostasis this does not happen everywhere and altogether? ⟦q20.1|54⟧ For we converge sometimes by these wills, though they are of inclination, both with God, and with angels, and with one another; but not for this have we by now fallen away from inclination and from the will according to it. ⟦q20.1|55⟧ For if we fell away from these, we would fall away also, plainly, from convergence with the others; since, as long as we have this, we hold to the other things of inclination too. ⟦q20.1|55⟧ If then this is so here, and no opposed array is seen at all, although the wills of inclination are preserved in existence and number, and keep the friendly disposition in relation: how, in the case of the same and one God the Word incarnate, who became man perfectly for us, will there be opposition in the wills that belong naturally to him according to essence? ⟦q20.1|56⟧ And how do they say these are contrary: because of number itself, or because of existing itself? ⟦q20.1|57⟧ If because of this, let them destroy the nature of the flesh as well, as opposed to the Word because of its existence. ⟦q20.1|58⟧ For the subject, as is plain to all, is much stronger toward opposed array than what is in a subject. ⟦q20.1|59⟧ But if because of number alone, let them make the natures bereft of this too, and let them not number even one of them; and let them refuse it again, for the same cause, in the case of the three hypostases of the one Godhead without beginning as well, lest that too be at strife with itself. ⟦q20.1|60⟧ But it is absurd to allot so great a strength to what is nothing at all (not essence, not quality, not any other of the things that are); and more absurd to blame the very existence of the natural will, and to contrive its destruction from that; and this, though among the others in the

⟦q21.1|1⟧ [0196] nature it is the very first, the innate power of natural idioms and motions. ⟦q21.1|2⟧ For according to it alone we naturally aspire to being and living, and moving, and understanding, and speaking, and perceiving, and partaking of food, of sleep and of rest, and to not being pained, nor dying; and, in a word, to having perfectly the habits of all that constitutes the nature, and the privations of what injures it.

⟦q21.1|3⟧ And the devout monk seems to me to have done fittingly in this too, in his definition, adding: «and holding together all the idioms that belong essentially to the nature.» ⟦q21.1|4⟧ For the essential and innate will in us always holds all things together, and aspires to hold them together, and to have privation of none; or rather we do so, both through it and according to it. ⟦q21.1|4⟧ And I think that whoever takes account in any degree of what is, and not of what seems, will altogether agree that God incarnate has, as man, a natural and human will; and that he wills each of the things enumerated naturally according to it, for confirmation of his flesh animated intellectually and rationally, if indeed he became perfect man, lacking none of what is ours, save only sin; but having all that we have, without omission — the things constitutive according to nature of our essence, as also those of the essence without beginning and divine, through which he made himself known as properly being man and God at once, and as preserving perfectly after the union the natural kindredness both toward us and toward the Father.

⟦q21.1|5⟧ For to say that only at the time of the saving passion, when he was really figuring what is ours in himself, and as man was refusing death, he was shown to have two wills, seems to me assertive, not demonstrative. ⟦q21.1|6⟧ For how only at this time, and not before it, and for what cause? ⟦q21.1|7⟧ For if he had it then, he had it also from the beginning, from when he became man, and did not improvise it later; but if he did not have it from the beginning, neither did he at the time of the passion, but he only feigned the refusal, and besides it the rest through which we have been saved: the tear, the prayer, the grief, the agony, the cross, the death, the burial. ⟦q21.1|7⟧ For if one of the natural things like ours that are preserved in him is annulled, neither will the others stand. ⟦q21.1|8⟧ For how would that not, and these would? ⟦q21.1|9⟧ And what is the account? ⟦q21.1|10+11+12+13⟧ And what kind of opposition is it to pray, and voluntarily to make a display of natural weakness — of the flesh's, I mean — through contraction, and not to resist at all, but to say, If it be possible, [n: Matth. xxvi, 39.] and, Not what I will, but whatever thou; and to join to the contraction the strong and eager movement against death? ⟦q21.1|10+11+12+13⟧ For he was really figuring what is ours in himself through the natural agony for a little while [?], [0197] so that he might free us from this too, and confirm the nature of his own flesh, and make the economy pure of phantasy for all; but at once he presented again the very great impulse against death, and the utmost growing-together and union of his human will with his own and the Father's will, by deciding for this and saying: Not mine, but thine be done: in this repelling division, in that, in turn, confusion.

⟦q21.1|10+11+12+13⟧ But if they say these things because he does not appear at other times to have made a reflection of willing, except at the time of the passion, let them say this also of his innate intellection, and they will make him mindless. ⟦q21.1|14⟧ For he does not appear to have said, I have understood. ⟦q21.1|15⟧ And likewise of smell, and of the rest of the natural things like ours, of which he is not recorded to have made mention. ⟦q21.1|16+17⟧ Or also of the soul itself, as not existing earlier in existence, from when he became man, but only when he made mention of it; for he is not declared to have done this long before the passion. ⟦q21.1|18⟧ Either then let them say that it was improvised at that moment, because he did not mention it often and from the beginning; or, not saying this, but teaching, with the great Athanasius, «At once flesh, at once flesh of God the Word, animate, rational», they will say that the Saviour's natural will as man was not only at the time of the passion, even if he then made mention of it, but co-essentiated with the Saviour from the beginning naturally, as with a man like us; and let them cease at last stirring up and gathering such things out of season. ⟦q21.1|19⟧ For the present time is one of blood, on account of our sins, and not of doctrines; and of strong lamentations, for the entreating of God, and not of sophistical rebuttals, to his greater indignation against us.

⟦q21.1|20+21+22+23+24+25⟧ It occurs to me also to wonder about operation: how they teach that this is one in Christ, by regard and reference, as they themselves say, to the individual. ⟦q21.1|20+21+22+23+24+25⟧ And — to pass over saying that none of the natural things has its reference primarily to the individual, but to its nature and essence; for who could operate anything without the powers that belong naturally to the nature? or whence has he operating or being able, apart from the nature? — this I wish to ask: was it, then, for the destruction of the natural operations that they put this forward, or for what? ⟦q21.1|20+21+22+23+24+25⟧ For if for destruction, how, when, and what kind of operation would there be at all upon their cessation? ⟦q21.1|20+21+22+23+24+25⟧ For as, natures not being, what is from them altogether vanishes as well — I mean the composite hypostasis — so too, the natural operations not being, there will be no other at all. ⟦q21.1|26⟧ And I do not say this as though another operation were constituted from the natural ones. ⟦q21.1|27⟧ For there is no composition at all of things in a subject, because it presents not their existence by themselves at all, but what is random. [0200] So that if they say that those which properly are according to nature are not, how will what in no way is exist? ⟦q21.1|28⟧ For who is there that speaks of a hypostatic operation, and whence is this, and from whom have they received it that they bring it forward?

⟦q21.1|29⟧ But if perhaps they declare this, and confess the hypostasis to which it is referred to be composite, and receptive of contraries and opposites: for this, in all likelihood, the operation too will be, mortal and immortal, visible and invisible, circumscribed and uncircumscribed, without beginning and under a beginning. ⟦q21.1|30⟧ And how otherwise than plainly by habit and privation? ⟦q21.1|30⟧ And the one set it will undergo as being, the other, as not being, it will bring into a parasitic subsistence. ⟦q21.1|31+32+33⟧ And how will he too who has such an operation not undergo like things, in the same respect and not in one and another, since neither does it? ⟦q21.1|31+32+33⟧ For it is altogether necessary that the one operating cleave connaturally to his own operation, and it to him. ⟦q21.1|31+32+33⟧ And how is he God who is so by habit, and not God by nature? ⟦q21.1|31+32+33⟧ And how man, who is this by privation, and not properly by essence? ⟦q21.1|31+32+33⟧ And what nature is without operation, or apart from natural operation? ⟦q21.1|34⟧ For as it is in no way bereft of existence, so neither of natural power. ⟦q21.1|35⟧ And if it has no share in this, it would have no share in existence either. ⟦q21.1|36+37+38⟧ For the powerless, as altogether inert, is only non-being. ⟦q21.1|36+37+38⟧ For every being whatever has a constitutive difference, the innate movement, taken together with the genus and making the definition of the subject, through which it is properly known that it is and what it is; having invariance toward things of the same species, and in turn difference toward things of other species. ⟦q21.1|36+37+38⟧ And if this is so, how is it possible for the Word become man, having no share of natural power according to the flesh, to be styled perfect man, or man at all? ⟦q21.1|36+37+38⟧ For man is not without natural operation, as neither is any other nature without its own and essential operation. ⟦q21.1|39⟧ For the lack of this either makes the nature not even to be a nature, or makes all natures the same as one another, and one in place of all, commixed and thoroughly confused by the failing of what constitutes them.

⟦q21.1|40⟧ For if they say that the Word is not without operation in what is ours, it is plain that they say he has an innate and human operation. ⟦q21.1|41⟧ For how else? ⟦q21.1|42⟧ For it is not possible to operate without natural operation, as neither to exist without essence and nature. ⟦q21.1|43⟧ But neither is it altogether necessary that the one operating have only one innate operation, if he be twofold in nature; otherwise the one subsisting will altogether have one nature too, even if he be composite in hypostasis. ⟦q21.1|44⟧ For if that follows because of the relation, this too altogether follows because of the same. ⟦q21.1|45+46⟧ For we see this relation borne singularly in both cases: for both the operation is referred back to the one operating, and the nature in turn to the one subsisting. ⟦q21.1|47⟧ They ought, then, if because of the union and the singleness of the person they say the operation of the one operating is one, for this reason to declare the nature too of the one subsisting one, lest they be caught teaching a relational union, saying it is a union of operations and not also of natures. ⟦q21.1|48⟧ For it is not possible for them to unite these to a hypostasis, but only to an essence and nature, so far as concerns the [0201] natural operations, these being united to it in nothing else but operation. ⟦q21.1|49⟧ Otherwise, as the natural difference of these, according to them, makes the operating ones two, so surely, altogether, will the innate otherness of the essences also infer the subsisting ones to be two. ⟦q21.1|50⟧ Either then let them commix these too into one nature; or let them in no way introduce division into those, which are essences naturally and are numbered. ⟦q21.1|51⟧ For what does not happen in those would never occur in these either. ⟦q21.1|52⟧ But the natural difference is shown through their number; and division departs through the utmost union. ⟦q21.1|53⟧ For it is not possible for a divine or human nature to be known even to be, let alone to differ from another, without essential operation. ⟦q21.1|54⟧ For the definition of the thing is properly the principle of its essential power; which being destroyed, the subject too is altogether destroyed with it. ⟦q21.1|55⟧ And for this reason we confess these preserved naturally in the Word incarnate: the one shown in the flesh's onset upon divine things, the other again in the experience of human things, with authority; so that, together with these, we may confess the natures too, whose essential operations they are.

⟦q21.1|56⟧ To whom is it not plain that in the case of one and the same essence and nature one cannot see a difference of existence or of natural operation? ⟦q21.1|57⟧ For none differs from itself; for this is somehow impossible. ⟦q21.1|58⟧ But in an individual and person, everywhere and altogether, if it be composite: for we see both the different natures from which it is constituted, and the natural movements by which, operating according to nature, it is known, confirming its own existence; and through the natural operation according to the one and the other it presents in fact that the things from which it is are other and other: through which we find that the assault of him who looks to it — I mean to the individual — and who willingly passes the natures by in order to destroy the natural operations, does not weigh upon us in this more than it works with us. ⟦q21.1|59⟧ For if the individual is not altogether enclosed in one natural operation, as indeed not in one nature either — for who says so, or is able to show it? — it is plain that it has essential movements equal in number to the natures, that is, the essences, in it; and no one will gainsay it. ⟦q21.1|60⟧ And I pass over saying that the composite person of Christ is not properly called an individual either. ⟦q21.1|61⟧ For it has no relation to the division that descends from the most generic genus through the subaltern genera to the most specific species,

⟦q22.1|1⟧ [0204] and circumscribing in it its own procession.

⟦q22.1|2⟧ Whence for this reason, according to the most wise Cyril, the name «Christ» has neither the force of a definition (for it is not a species predicated of many that differ in number), nor indeed does it signify the essence of anything. ⟦q22.1|3⟧ For it is not an individual, referred up to a species or a genus, or circumscribed by these according to essence; but a composite hypostasis, identifying in itself to the utmost the natural division of the extremes, and leading them into one by the union of its own parts.

⟦q22.1|4⟧ But if perhaps, against those who, through the Lord's not being without operation according to the flesh, confess his innate and human operation, they infer that he is not without hypostasis either, this is to do violence to the hypostasis. ⟦q22.1|5+6⟧ For the nature's not being without hypostasis does not make it to be a hypostasis; just as neither does the body's not being shapeless make every body whatever a shape; nor does the begotten's or the visible's not being unbegotten or not invisible make it begetting or sight. ⟦q22.1|7⟧ And simply, anything else whatever, being denied to be that which it is not, makes the thing affirmed, that about which the relation itself is naturally contemplated, to be a relation.

⟦q22.1|8⟧ Besides, even if this perhaps were so, when it is not, it would be nothing to the natural operations, which are not rejected by the holy Fathers equally with the hypostases, nor do they divide the one into two, as the hypostases do. ⟦q22.1|9⟧ For who is he that could show that it is not good, for the declaration of the essential difference, to hold the natural operations in the same [Christ]? ⟦q22.1|9⟧ And for what reason, and how, and what is the word that lays this down, or what approved Father, so that they may not merely pronounce it? ⟦q22.1|10⟧ For if it is not good to confess the natural operations, neither is it to confess the natures themselves. ⟦q22.1|11⟧ Or how these, but not those? ⟦q22.1|12⟧ But if both are good, for what reason do they range themselves against the good, and construct the hypostasis from the nature's not being without hypostasis?

⟦q22.1|13⟧ For if they knew that the operations, like the hypostases, were censured by the Fathers, not unreasonably would they cast them away. ⟦q22.1|14⟧ But if we know the Fathers proclaiming these, and confessing a divine and a human operation, as indeed also a nature, and exhorting us to confess them, but a divine or human hypostasis no one in any way whatever, except only the divided and man-worshipping Nestorius, why then, if the teaching of the saints is not to be rejected by them, do they play the sophist in such ways against it, toward the removal of the natural operations? ⟦q22.1|15⟧ And whose is it to infer these things, and thus, except Severus, the sophist and deranged, who zealously destroys his own positions and in no way prevails over the pious, because of the conspicuousness of the truth, even if he contrives with the shameless manner of sophistical drivel?

⟦q22.1|15⟧ [0205] For if the nature's not being without hypostasis makes it a hypostasis, then surely, by consequence, the hypostasis's not being without essence presents it as an essence. ⟦q22.1|16⟧ And those who say this, how do they not divide the account of theology by natures equal in number to the hypostases, if indeed, according to them, the hypostases that are not without essence are altogether essences; and how do they not confuse that of the economy, by the singleness of the nature on account of the one hypostasis? ⟦q22.1|17⟧ Being consistent with themselves, then, they will make doctrines of such things against themselves too.

⟦q22.1|18⟧ But for those who confess the natural operations, even to infer them thus is a grace, since through all things they gather the truth for themselves according to the tradition of the saints; according to which the not-without-hypostasis does not make the nature to be a hypostasis, but enhypostatic, so that it may not be taken as an accident by conception alone, but may be contemplated really as a form. ⟦q22.1|19⟧ So too the not-without-essence does not make the hypostasis an essence, but presents it as enousial, so that we may know it not as a bare idiom, but properly together with that in which the idiom is. ⟦q22.1|20+21⟧ As then there the enhypostatic signifies the enhyparctic, and the enhyparctic is that which partakes of essential and natural existence; so here too the operant, that is, the operative, properly signifies the potent; and the potent is that which has essential and natural power.

⟦q22.1|22⟧ Therefore to confess that the natures in Christ are not without hypostasis or without operation is not to infer hypostases or operating subjects, but to confess orthodoxly their essential and natural existences and operations, unto a true assurance and confirmation of God the Word incarnate, who is from them and in them and according to them (I mean the natures), operating according to the indissoluble union.

⟦q22.1|23⟧ And by what reason do they themselves, referring many operations, more than number, to one and the same, by saying «every divine and human operation» (for «every» suggests that we think not one nor two at all, nor three, [0208] but infinite operations exceeding number, just as also «all things» and «everything» do) in no way suspect division; and yet this division, against those who confess the natural two according to the divine Fathers, to the extent of knowing the difference, they infer, I know not how or why, along with the operating subjects? ⟦q22.1|24⟧ For if the many and different by nature do not divide and cut the one and the same into many operating subjects, how do the two? ⟦q22.1|25⟧ And who is it that rather blames the assault as unreasonable? ⟦q22.1|26⟧ For if they did not say and refer these as to the same and of the same, and as grown together with one another by the interpenetration toward one another through the whole, and preserved in him according to the indissoluble union, and moved by him, yet according to one thing and another, uniformly; through which he both put forth divinely the power of the miracles, as God by nature, and willingly accepted the experience of the sufferings for us, as man by nature; then their assault would after all not be implausible. ⟦q22.1|27⟧ But as it is, since they do not say this, but refer these as of the same and lead them up to the same, whence and how do they infer the operating subjects? ⟦q22.1|28⟧ And why, if it is reasonable, do they not first infer the many against themselves, so far as concerns the many operations? ⟦q22.1|29⟧ For as the two infer the two, so surely of necessity the many infer the many. ⟦q22.1|30⟧ But if not even this, how that follows I cannot see; and I think neither can anyone else of those who prefer the truth to seeming.

⟦q22.1|30⟧ And how again is the one put forward by them, as if contradictorily, in his case against the many? ⟦q22.1|31⟧ And what is this one, and how do they make it a doctrine? ⟦q22.1|32⟧ For it is not possible for the same to have both many and one. ⟦q22.1|33⟧ For if many, not one; and if one, how again many? ⟦q22.1|34⟧ For either that one will altogether thrust these away, or these will thrust that one away, according to their own account, by which they put forward the one for the overthrow of the natural two. ⟦q22.1|35⟧ But if perhaps they say that the one is produced from many, it is not the nature, as has been said, of things in a subject to be compounded. ⟦q22.1|36⟧ Nor again is it the nature of the one from many to be cut up into many. ⟦q22.1|37+38⟧ Let them then first themselves destroy the more through the one operation, lest they introduce many operating subjects against themselves; and through the many, again, the one, lest they make a doctrine of co-essentiation; and so let them make war on those who hold the two, if indeed they are not feeble. ⟦q22.1|39⟧ For it is utterly impossible for those who overthrow themselves together with their own positions, needing nothing else at all for this, to attack others. ⟦q22.1|40⟧ For that which has a base, that which is, is firmly founded by it, [0209] suffering on no side any downfall whatever. ⟦q22.1|41⟧ For that alone is truly vigorous and strong which has the truth confirming it; but not that which receives its dissolution from another, let alone from itself.

⟦q22.1|42⟧ Having let these men go, then, it befits us altogether, being pious, and confessing really the existence of each of the natures preserved in the one God the Word incarnate, to confess altogether also the innate operation, as also the will; and through the pious and most true belief concerning each, the divine together with the human, of the same, and concerning the things belonging to him naturally, to make firm the orthodox teaching of the holy Fathers, who handed down to the holy catholic Churches of God both to think and to say these things about him; and to deny nothing of what he was from the beginning and became later for us, nor of all the idioms that characterize these naturally. ⟦q22.1|43⟧ For they taught that the same is perfect in both, and in both natures, and preserving without lack the natural property of each of those from which he was, and in all things like us, and likewise tempted according to volition, without sin alone.

⟦q22.1|44⟧ Hearing, then, these blessed voices, we deny utterly nothing in his case, neither of what is ours and natural, nor of what is the Father's and divine; but we hold that the same has different natures and natural operations and essential wills, that is, two. ⟦q22.1|45⟧ For neither our nature nor indeed the Father's stands outside essence, or without innate will or operation. ⟦q22.1|46+47+48⟧ And if this is so, it is clear that he who subsisted from these, I mean Godhead and manhood, according to one and the same hypostasis, and who is properly the same God and man, is consubstantial with us and of the same operation, having the same innate volition according to the manhood; and consubstantial with the Father and of the same operation, possessing the same volition according to the Godhead; and wholly akin to those above in all that is akin, and wholly akin to those below in all that is akin, without sin alone. ⟦q22.1|46+47+48⟧ And he who does not so hold does not receive the God-inspired Fathers who proclaim these things; but it is clear whom he receives, and of what sort.

⟦q22.1|46+47+48⟧ May it be granted that all of us, by the mediation of your God-wrought prayers, guarded always in soul and body in the temple of God, the catholic and apostolic Church, may hold this sincere and orthodox confession of our great God and Saviour before him and all creation, and in place of all things and before all things [0212] may bring it to him at his all-glorious appearing; and may be deemed worthy by him of the like portion, and partake of the everlasting and blessed gifts promised to those who confess him unashamedly before men, and enjoy in experience, according to our prayers, those which we have now tasted, deemed worthy by faith; by the prayers and intercessions of our all-glorious and all-holy, immaculate Lady, the Theotokos and Ever-Virgin Mary, and of all the saints. ⟦q22.1|46+47+48⟧ Amen.

⟦q22.1|46+47+48⟧ SCHOLIA

⟦q22.2|1⟧ 1. ⟦q22.3|1+2⟧ The invisible is the negation of what is seen. ⟦q22.3|1+2⟧ But what is affirmed is not affirmed toward sight; rather, the visible is affirmed; which is the relation of the not-invisible, that is, that toward which the not-invisible has its relation. ⟦q22.3|3⟧ And of sight, the relation toward which it is affirmed is not the not-invisible, but blindness.

⟦q22.4|1+2+3+4+5⟧ 2. ⟦q22.4|1+2+3+4+5⟧ Against those who say that there is no nature without hypostasis, one must convert it: neither is there a hypostasis without essence. ⟦q22.4|1+2+3+4+5⟧ Then one must add that, just as saying that the hypostasis is not without essence does not present it as being an essence (for so it would be to divide and to confuse, the one the mystery of theology, the other that of the economy), so neither does saying that the nature is not without hypostasis introduce the hypostasis.

⟦q23.1|1+2+3⟧ OPUSCULUM 17 — ON THE DEFINITIONS OF THE DEMARCATIONS

⟦q23.1|1+2+3⟧ *Of the same. ⟦q23.1|1+2+3⟧ On the definitions of demarcations.*

⟦q23.1|1+2+3⟧ Every demarcation of the things that are comes about either according to opposition, or according to being set against, that is, being divided or being destroyed. ⟦q23.1|1+2+3⟧ For instance, sensibles and intelligibles have opposition as being of other essence, but they are not set against one another. ⟦q23.1|4⟧ For these are not destructive of one another; and for this reason they admit toward themselves the demarcation according to opposition, that is, division. ⟦q23.1|4⟧ But life and death have a demarcation, yet not according to opposition, but that of being set against, that is, the destructive. ⟦q23.1|5⟧ For the positing of the one becomes the removal of the other; and the existence of the one is the non-existence of the other. ⟦q23.1|6⟧ If then anything is antecedently set against another, it is consequently altogether also opposed to it; but if anything is antecedently opposed to another, it is consequently in no way set against it. ⟦q23.1|6⟧ And to say it more clearly: the essences, through the differences that are in them, admit the demarcation toward one another according to opposition, as soul and body, and all things that differ according to essence; but the things about the essence, that is, qualities, have the demarcation according to being set against, as life and death, and anything of the sort. ⟦q23.1|7⟧ For these are manifestly destructive of one another.

⟦q23.2|1⟧ [0213] SCHOLION

⟦q23.2|1⟧ By opposition understand division; by being set against, think destruction. ⟦q23.2|2⟧ For the one of them is in the essences; the other is inborn in the things about the essence. ⟦q23.2|2⟧ And in the latter both run together wonderfully, destruction and division again; but the former, again, avails to divide, not to destroy.

⟦q23.2|3⟧ *Definitions of unions.*

⟦q23.2|4⟧ Union according to essence: in the case of hypostases, that is, of individuals.

⟦q23.2|5⟧ Union according to hypostasis: in the case of essences, that is, of soul and body.

⟦q23.2|6⟧ Union according to relation: in the case of inclinations, into one will.

⟦q23.2|7⟧ Union according to juxtaposition: in the case of planks.

⟦q23.2|8⟧ Union according to fitting: in the case of stones.

⟦q23.2|9⟧ Union according to blending: in the case of liquids, wine and water, and the like.

⟦q23.2|10⟧ Union according to commixture: in the case of dry things and liquids, flour and water.

⟦q23.2|10⟧ Union according to confusion: in the case of meltable things, wax and pitch, and the like.

⟦q23.2|11⟧ Union according to heaping: in the case of dry things, wheat and barley, and the like.

⟦q23.2|12⟧ Union according to fusion: in the case of things drawn off [0216] and restored again; as a torch going forth from fire and restored to it again.

⟦q23.2|13+14⟧ Union according to essence is in the case of things of other hypostasis.

⟦q23.3|1+2+3+4⟧ Union according to hypostasis is in the case of things of other essence.

⟦q23.3|1+2+3+4⟧ OPUSCULUM 18 — THE DIFFICULTIES OF THEODORE OF BYZANTIUM, AND THE FIRST SOLUTION

⟦q23.3|1+2+3+4⟧ *First difficulty of Theodore, deacon of Byzantium, rhetor and synodicarius of Paul, archbishop of Constantinople.*

⟦q23.3|1+2+3+4⟧ If the Fathers predicated ignorance also of Christ, according to the same principle as the will, it is necessary that those who say that the will is contemplated in Christ not according to appropriation also say that he is ignorant. ⟦q23.3|1+2+3+4⟧ And how is he God who does not know the things to come? ⟦q23.3|5⟧ Rather, they must also introduce him as a mere man, according to the already condemned heresy of the Agnoetae. ⟦q23.3|6⟧ But if, shrinking from the absurdity that follows from this, they say the ignorance is according to appropriation, as also the forsakenness and the insubordination, let them in like manner say the will too. ⟦q23.3|7⟧ For the Fathers ranked ignorance together with the will, keeping for it the same principle; as Athanasius says in his book against the Arians, and Gregory the Theologian in the first oration on the Son, and others in other writings.

⟦q23.3|8⟧ *Second difficulty of the same.*

⟦q23.3|9⟧ If every expression and word not spoken by the Fathers [0217] is manifestly innovation, one of two things is necessary: either to show that it is a word of the Fathers, to speak of natural wills in Christ; or, not being able to show it, let them know that under the name of the Fathers they are innovating their own teaching. ⟦q23.3|10⟧ But if, understanding other words of theirs toward their own aim, they make such a coinage of names: if the second is blameworthy, then the first is too.

⟦q23.3|11⟧ *Of Maximus the monk, to Marinus the presbyter: Solution of the difficulties set out above by Theodore, deacon and rhetor. ⟦q23.3|11⟧ Solution 1.*

⟦q23.3|12⟧ The rhetor's inquiries, most holy and God-honoured father, do not so much have the reasonableness of a difficulty as display the unreasonableness of the difficulty, both from his falsehood and from his strife for the overthrow of the divine incarnation of the Only-begotten. ⟦q23.3|13⟧ For not according to the same principle as the will, as he says, did the Fathers predicate ignorance of Christ. ⟦q23.3|14⟧ For who is he that can show this, even if these men, as is their custom, not only fabricate things that in no way are, but also boldly bring them forward against themselves? ⟦q23.3|15⟧ Or else in this way the principle of ignorance and of will will be the same, bringing into the same things that are in every way properly incompatible with one another; since the one presents the destruction of what is, the other the positing of what is; which is plainly impossible, as habit and privation are not at all the same with one another.

⟦q23.3|16⟧ For it is not because the divine and great Gregory counts this too among the words put forward by the Arians (I mean ignorance) that he has thereby declared it the same as the will. ⟦q23.3|17+18+19⟧ For he did not even expressly mention the will at all in the place where he goes through these things, saying: «But do thou count for me against these the words of their ingratitude: 'My God and your God'; [n: Joan. x, 36.] 'greater'; 'he created'; 'he sanctified'», and what follows. ⟦q23.3|17+18+19⟧ But if, because he only took it along with these, they perhaps attempt to bring ignorance into the same with the will, then surely 'My God and your God', and 'greater', said of the Father, and to create and to sanctify, will also be the same as ignorance according to them, because of the ranking-together, since he ranked these too together with ignorance. ⟦q23.3|17+18+19⟧ But if this is a perversion of understanding, that is still more an aberration of those who reason [0220] thus, let alone of those who attempt to say or write it.

⟦q23.3|17+18+19⟧ Besides, if the principle of ignorance and of will is the same, either the things that will by nature are altogether also ignorant, or the things that are ignorant by nature altogether also will. ⟦q23.3|17+18+19⟧ So then God too, willing according to nature, will fall into the passion of ignorance; and all inanimate things, ignorant according to nature, will be moved by a natural will. ⟦q23.3|20⟧ And if this is so, Christ himself too, who subsisted from Godhead and manhood, having, as they say, both the ignorance and the will according to appropriation, will surely have by nature, in respect of the divine will, the ignorance as well. ⟦q23.3|21⟧ And I keep silent about saying that, even if erroneously because of the appropriation, they were nevertheless shown to be advocates of that which they fought against, themselves also constituting two wills, the one according to nature, I mean, and the one according to appropriation; which are manifestly two, with ignorances equal in number; which is also absurd, to opine a twofold ignorance in Christ, who has not even one, but altogether makes every ignorance vanish, if indeed he is the wisdom and power of God. [n: I Cor. i, 24.]

⟦q23.3|22⟧ And what appropriation, then, do they mean? ⟦q23.3|23⟧ The essential, according to which each thing makes its own what belongs to it naturally, as having it through its nature; or the relational, according to which we naturally love and make our own one another's things, while we ourselves neither suffer nor operate any of them? ⟦q23.3|24⟧ But if the first, they rather declared the incarnate God a mere man, making a doctrine of him as naturally ignorant. ⟦q23.3|25⟧ But if the second, not a man at all, but only God without flesh, as having in a mere relation the things of the flesh, and not as making his own naturally the natural things of a man, together with being and remaining God. ⟦q23.3|26⟧ Since in the place where he puts ignorance, the teacher also clearly enumerates all the natural things of God incarnate: such as sleeping, hungering, thirsting, toiling, weeping, being in agony, shrinking back, the cross, death; and with these the rising too, and the taking-up, by which he also saved us, having willingly accepted these things according to nature for us, and suffered. ⟦q23.3|27⟧ If then they opine that these took place in Christ according to the mere appropriation in relation, and do not hold them to be natural to the incarnate Word as man, what is left for them to leave to Apollinarius and Manes? ⟦q23.3|28⟧ For they have surpassed Severus in impiety, shouting louder in blasphemy, since he did not rise up so much against the truth, [0221] but made use of the expression of the natural idioms, even if he sets aside the nature of the flesh, squeezing the different natures into one.

⟦q23.3|29+30+31⟧ For these things will follow those who mix the unmixable, and who thrust away and steal aside the mind of the Fathers, which sets all things distinctly in fitting order. ⟦q23.3|29+30+31⟧ Whence the all-praised and God-bearing teacher, in the second oration on the Son, goes through these things about ignorance, defining as by a rule and pattern, though these men at first brought them forward to us uninterpreted, snatching away the hearer: «Is it not plain to all, that he knows as God, but says he is ignorant as man, if one separates what appears from what is understood?» ⟦q23.3|29+30+31⟧ He lays down, then, that ignorance is to be taken only in the demarcation of the essences in conception; but that the natural wills, and everything else natural except sin alone, are to be known in the hypostatic union of the natures of the incarnate Word himself, unto the most proper confirmation of those from which and in which he is, and which he is.

⟦q23.3|32⟧ If then we contemplate ignorance in the demarcation in conception, it is not properly his; it is what is not ours either according to nature as constitutive, even if it is said to be ours because of the ancient transgression, such as ignorance, forsakenness, disobedience, insubordination. ⟦q23.3|33⟧ For what principle of nature is there for us in these, or what is constitutive of the essence? ⟦q23.3|34⟧ Wherefore we do not predicate these of Christ either, as being at all Christ's, nor anything else, as I said, of the things known in the demarcation in conception; and very reasonably. ⟦q23.3|35⟧ For if we do not know the incarnate Word in demarcation, nor imagine him in bare conception, but believe him to be in the utmost hypostatic union of the natures, it is clear that nothing of the things taken in division in conception is his at all; but those things alone we hold properly his, whatever are natural together with the nature, and are outside the defilement that comes by sin, being preserved really, in the unity of person and of his one hypostasis, and confessed by the pious.

⟦q23.3|35⟧ So then, if the will is not natural and constitutive of our essence, let them prove it. ⟦q23.3|36⟧ But if it is natural (for man is volitive by nature), either it too, along with the other things of the incarnate Word, both is and is known according to essence, as of one who became man and is volitive according to both the natures from which he is composed; or the other natural things too, along with it (I mean the will according to nature), will be known in the division in conception, barely, but not properly and really; and according to them the contemplation and initiation of the whole economy, or rather the incarnation itself, properly, will be taken by them according to division and imagination, [0224] nothing natural being confessed by them in Christ according to union, nor the nature itself at all. ⟦q23.3|37+38⟧ For where there is nothing natural, neither will there ever be a nature; just as, where properly there is the nature, there are the things according to it without omission, except sin alone. ⟦q23.3|39⟧ Wherefore no pretext at all will they have against the truth. ⟦q23.3|40⟧ For every conception, and through all things, has been taken away, so that they may, if indeed they should wish, gladly welcome the truth and sincerely confess with us, and not lean on the arguments, spun as from a spider, of their feeble understanding: with us, those who believe in him orthodoxly, that from what Christ is, in these Christ is; and in what Christ is, these Christ is. ⟦q23.3|41⟧ Christ, then, is God and man in the same; and he is both in Godhead and in manhood; and he was united both from Godhead and from manhood.

⟦q24.1|1⟧ OPUSCULUM 19 — SOLUTION OF THE SECOND ABSURDITY

⟦q24.1|2+3⟧ *Of the same holy Maximus, solution of the second absurdity.*

⟦q24.1|2+3⟧ As for showing that the Fathers speak of natural wills, we shall show it then, when they are able to hearken, or, to put it more properly, are willing to, who have stopped up the hearing power of the soul toward all divine and Fatherly things. ⟦q24.1|4⟧ For if they are willing to cleanse this, as from some passion, of strife, and clearly, or rather without passionate attachment, to fix the eye of the understanding according to reason on the many-lit and holy radiance of the teaching handed down from the Fathers, they will surely understand that they name natural wills; both from the fact that all expressly say and teach in common: «A natural appetite, and an innate appetite, and the flesh's having naturally the not willing to die; for this is a condemnation of nature. ⟦q24.1|5⟧ And how, he says, is the will common to both? ⟦q24.1|5⟧ And where would the difference of nature be shown by the one will? ⟦q24.1|6⟧ For there is every necessity that the wish concur with the nature, and that the nature not wish to die, but cling to the present life. ⟦q24.1|7⟧ And just as hungering and thirsting is no charge, so neither is aspiring to the present life. ⟦q24.1|8⟧ And that the fondness for things present lies in nature.» ⟦q24.1|9⟧ And: «The flesh which he assumed for us having, with all the things that belong to it naturally, also the deprecating of death.» ⟦q24.1|10⟧ These are the doctrines of Athanasius, and Basil, and Gregory, and John, and Theophilus, and Cyril, the ever-memorable, and of the other approved Fathers.

⟦q24.1|11⟧ What greater demonstration than these, that they declare natural wills? ⟦q24.1|12⟧ And what contradiction, and from whom, when there is no ambiguity in these sayings, or any concealment whatever? ⟦q24.1|13⟧ We then do not on this account innovate expressions, as the adversaries say, but confess Fatherly words; nor do we, understanding them toward our own aim, make coinages of names; for this is a bold thing, and the work [0225] of a heretical and mad understanding, an invention; but as they were understood and spoken by the saints themselves, so we piously bring them forward.

⟦q24.1|14⟧ But what room, and whence, will they have for not altogether understanding thus, but opining otherwise and contrary to these, if indeed they wish at all to be pious, and not to swerve from the Fatherly teaching? ⟦q24.1|15⟧ For if they will altogether have hypostatic, that is, personal wills, shrinking from the natural ones, they will also introduce personal otherness; or if wills contrary to nature, they also make a doctrine of the falling-away of the essences, driving the natures, or rather themselves, into non-existence. ⟦q24.1|16⟧ For there is nothing of the personal, nor of those contrary to nature, if indeed we shall at all grant that any such thing may be said, since it is not known at all, after the departure of the wills according to essence. ⟦q24.1|17⟧ For whether divine and human, or deifying and deified, or created and uncreated, or however else they may wish to call them, we are led piously to name the wills in Christ natural, and not otherwise, wishing to signify the natural otherness of those from which he is composed. ⟦q24.1|18⟧ For if, according to the Fathers, there is every necessity that the wish concur with the nature, and it is not possible to call the will common, plainly, to both the essences, nor is the difference of nature shown by the one will, it is clear that it is altogether necessary to say two natural wills; so that, according to the Fathers, the difference of the essences in Christ may be known without confusion and without division.

⟦q24.1|19⟧ What account, then, have they against these things, or what coinage of names would they fashion for themselves, understanding the Fatherly words toward their own aim? ⟦q24.1|20⟧ And what personal wills would they wish the pious man to choose, falling away from the natural wills? ⟦q24.1|21⟧ And who will bear the cutting-up of the one into persons? ⟦q24.1|22⟧ But the wills contrary to nature? ⟦q24.1|23⟧ And who would bear the pit of vice and of non-existence into which they drive themselves little by little? ⟦q24.1|24⟧ Only, for this we owe them thanks: that, having let go of fighting the number of the wills, they have turned to the natural account of them; which perhaps we too concede, that they name them as they wish, falling away in this respect from the number, until, learning the absurdity from the incongruous appellation, and to what unprofitable end it would lead them, they too should be persuaded to confess natural wills according to the Fathers.

⟦q24.1|25⟧ And who would not marvel also at their shifting about? ⟦q24.1|26⟧ How, using certain circuits and double courses, they contrive to remove the great mystery of the divine economy toward us? ⟦q24.1|27+28⟧ For at one time, bringing the will against the natural operations, through one, as they say, introducing for themselves the one operation, they went after the two operations; at another time bringing ignorance against the wills, as though the will departed together with it, and were reckoned among the things outside; at another time [bringing] the complete [0228] negation of all the natural things, through the appropriation, and, as I said, the whole overthrow of the mystery; all these being taken by them in his case in a mere relation, and not known naturally, according to union, as belonging to God incarnate as man; when they ought not to stand outside the truth, but rather to converge with it through all things and advocate it, according to the assurance, leaving nothing out, of the natures of the same and one Christ our God, and of the things belonging to the natures: a sincere and most firm confession; to whom be glory, with the Father and the Holy Spirit, unto the boundless ages. ⟦q24.1|29⟧ Amen.

⟦q25.1|1+2+3+4⟧ OPUSCULUM 20 — DOGMATIC TOME TO MARINUS THE PRESBYTER

⟦q25.1|1+2+3+4⟧ *Of Maximus, the most holy monk and confessor.*

⟦q25.1|1+2+3+4⟧ Those whose reason is adorned with virtues have their mind illumined with graces of knowledge, which remove it from all forgetfulness, as those [virtues] remove the reason from all deceit, and which lead it to initiation into the hidden so far as they lead it back to the imitation of him who was manifested for our sake. ⟦q25.1|1+2+3+4⟧ This thou hast shown forth in the Holy Spirit, in the things in which thou thyself, contending with diligence in holy manners and holier practices, God-honoured Father, surpassest praise as far as thou art able to bring vigour to bear: making the stretching-forth the end of the course, and the ever-moving ascent to the Word the end of reason, so that thou mayest pass beyond nature, and cast away all types, and converse purely with the most pure, according to the withdrawal of thyself from all things and toward all, and the perfect passing-over in relation; which procures the unbounded good flow by grace, a flow that circumscribes and passes beyond everything definable, and that [0229] prepares the one who ascends with him from glory to glory practically through virtue, and passes through the heavens from knowledge to knowledge scientifically, [n: Hebr. iv, 14.] and in the hidden-mystery and most-voiced silence, in the failing of all intellectual operation, meets unknowingly with the Father of spirits, [n: Hebr. xii, 9.] to advance tenaciously into the Holy of holies, where Jesus, he who is above us, entered as forerunner on our behalf in what is ours.

⟦q25.1|1+2+3+4⟧ To him, having been led up above nature by grace and by the blessed zeal for sacred rites, thou leadest up those like me who lie below, stretching out a hand with compassion through thy letter, and a word through the meaning hidden in it; and persuading [us] by zeal to withdraw from the things that remove [us] from God, and, without relation, [to honour, to practise, to prefer] from the whole heart only the love that is his and that unites to him; according to which, as simple and formless, thou exhortest [us] to beat off all doubleness, and the covering of figure both of the understanding itself and of what appears, and to achieve the virtue that is truly blessed and reveals the blessed things; which may I be able to accomplish, supported by thy most holy prayers, having put off the sin that so easily besets. [n: Ibid. 2.]

⟦q25.1|1+2+3+4⟧ But since, rousing my thought again to work and wishing to cleanse the capacity of my soul of all ignorance, thou hast bidden me search out the discourse against the Diaetetes of the most divine and great Teacher of the holy Church of the Theopolitans, which is the same as to say, of the whole inhabited world, who bears the name of the resurrection—or rather, to speak properly, against the Diaeretes, inasmuch as he separates entirely from God and from us, through utter conversion and commixture, God who became man for our sake, the offspring consubstantial with the Father, our Lord Jesus Christ—how he says the operation in him is one, although he teaches the operations as natural: I put forward the man himself as the wise interpreter and teacher of this phrase as his own, who defines it to be nothing other than the indissoluble union of the innate operations, and the result from them—I mean the work and the action—as being indicative and declarative of them; [an action] which has shared in the name of operation, if anything has, since it is properly something both particular and universal. ⟦q25.1|1+2+3+4⟧ For inasmuch as it is produced from the essential operation that is according to natural property, it holds the rank of a particular; just as, again, that [operation], producing [it], [holds the rank] of a universal. ⟦q25.1|5⟧ For it is natural that specific things share in the name of the generic, and that universal things be predicated of the [0232] existence of the particulars, yet not share their name, lest through this they be made specific somehow and reckoned in the rank of a particular.

⟦q25.1|6⟧ So the teacher, naming the result, as I said, of the two innate operations themselves, that is, the action, from the name that belongs to them, since it has gathered both together according to their union, called it one operation, because nothing divine or human is accomplished separately, but is produced from one and the same at once by growing-together and in a united way, according to the single interpenetration in these. ⟦q25.1|6⟧ Yet he did not for this reason call his essential operation one according to natural property; just as [he did not call his] essence and nature one on account of the very singleness of the one person, [an essence] sharing in neither of those from which he was composed. ⟦q25.1|7⟧ For even in the union of the natures the difference according to essence is preserved, preserving properly together with itself that also of the things that belong essentially to the natures.

⟦q25.1|8⟧ And declaring this in the very discourse he laboured over against the Diaetetes, after first demarcating very scientifically concerning natural property, which is fitly disposed toward operation, and concerning operation itself, which proceeds from the fitness into work and action, he goes through it thus: «Therefore one operation in Christ»—and he adds the cause, because nothing divine or human is accomplished separately—«we too say.» ⟦q25.1|9⟧ And when he had shown in this way the principle of the union, adding that of the essential difference, he says: «But not his property one, God forbid; for the property of Godhead and of manhood is not the same»—that is, the property fitly disposed toward natural operation, according to him. ⟦q25.1|10⟧ And going on again: «And otherwise, to say the same universally: that the operation of any natures whatever that have come together is one, by the communion, namely, of the union and of its completion, is wholly necessary; but to call their property one, without confusion—perhaps even in those where room for confusion is altogether inconceivable.»

⟦q25.1|11⟧ So the Father has most plainly made clear his own meaning by his own words, saying that the operation in Christ is not one essential [operation], lest confusion and mixing be brought upon his parts; nor hypostatic at all, lest separation and distance of the extremes happen to him—I mean of the Father without beginning and of the undefiled mother. ⟦q25.1|12⟧ For he is clearly distinguished from them by hypostasis and by the hypostatic [marks]. ⟦q25.1|13⟧ But what does he say? ⟦q25.1|14⟧ One by the communion of the union and of its completion, which he defined above as work and action; giving no entry of any kind either to those who wish to commix or to those who wish to separate; but driving off the former by making known what is principally and according to nature proper to his manhood, and what to the superessential Godhead; and routing the latter by setting apart or separating no operation in the one and the same.

⟦q25.1|15⟧ For the name of operation, taken in common of simple movement alike and [0233] of movement considered in relation, or of the result itself, brings no mixing upon the realities, so long as the clarification of the things understood is made distinct; by which we keep without division the things of either of the natures that have essentially run together, and recognize without confusion the things proper to the one as [the things] of the other, because of the union, and [the things] of both as [the things] of the whole from them, because of [its] existence; neither commixing [them] as to this [whole], nor separating [it] as to those [natures]; but holding the difference by the definitions of the essences, and binding tight the union by the principles of one and the same hypostasis. ⟦q25.1|16⟧ Thus then, and for these reasons, this man, and every other approved and divine Father, if there is any, [says], as I seem already to have written, that the operation in Christ is one and two: the one, looking to the union of the natural operations, as also of the natures; the other, to their essential difference. ⟦q25.1|17+18+19+20⟧ So much for this.

⟦q25.1|17+18+19+20⟧ As for the interpretation of the passage of Gregory, the theologian and great herald of the [truth of the] Church, the passage that says «the word is formed from the man, not from him who is understood in the Saviour; for his willing is not even opposed to God, being wholly deified», I determine it to depend on a very pious understanding, for which and out of which the industrious man laboured, but to be lacking a little in precision of wording; against which those who are facile and gross in everything contrive to make raids, or, to say it more truly, onslaughts. ⟦q25.1|17+18+19+20⟧ And nothing is so earnestly sought by them as to search out and find fault with even the things very securely guarded on every side and opening no room to them against the account; let alone when they have seized from somewhere upon a bare word, and that one put forward from an unfeigned and pure understanding: that this God-possessed Father, for confirming and establishing the essential and natural will of the ensouled flesh assumed from us (that is, of the human [element] in the Saviour), says: «For his willing is wise and circumspect», from which its essential difference from his will that is by nature divine and paternal is shown, a difference allowing no confusion at all to grow upon the mystery of Christ.

⟦q25.1|17+18+19+20⟧ But to pronounce *wholly deified* with paroxytone accent, as from certain copies, and not rather with oxytone, for fear lest the one will be brought in by the opponents, gives a handle against us, as though we were hinting at a relational and hypostatic union, as it were by grace and worth, in that the saints too are [0236] principally moved and operated by God, because of their whole convergence and disposition toward God and divine things. ⟦q25.1|17+18+19+20⟧ For the oxytone expression of *deified* neither brings into identity with the essential and natural will the willing of the Saviour as man (for who is able to show it?), and it presents the utmost union and growing-together. ⟦q25.1|21⟧ For *deified*, being among the relatives, as also *ignited* and *illumined* and all such, by all means brings in with itself also that to which it has the reference of its relation, that is, the deifying, and the igniting, and the illumining; so that the principle both of the difference and of the utmost union is made firm no more from that side than from this. ⟦q25.1|22⟧ For not to be opposed is not sufficient for union; for everything that is natural and blameless is not contrary, but neither is it by all means also united to God. ⟦q25.1|23⟧ But what is deified is united in every way and wholly, and in no way displaces the essential difference, inasmuch as it is unconfused in the union.

⟦q25.1|24⟧ But if someone will say: "And if nothing natural is opposed, how is it that the Father, making mention of our innate will—if indeed it was of that, and not perhaps of some other—did not say that it follows God by all means, but that it is «resisting for the most part and wrestling against»? ⟦q25.1|25⟧ For either it is not natural, as being adverse, or it is not adverse, as being natural. ⟦q25.1|26+27+28+29⟧ And then it stands as other in natural quality from the willing of the human [element] in the Saviour, if this is in no way [opposed], but that is opposed": we say that insofar as it is natural, it is not opposed; but insofar as it is not moved naturally by us, it is clearly opposed, and resisting for the most part, whereupon sinning also follows. ⟦q25.1|26+27+28+29⟧ For it is in the mode of the movement according to misuse, and not in the principle of the power according to nature, that what is contrary to reason and law subsists; since, when it is shaped and moved suitably, even if it does not have being-united to God, it has at least concurring and not resisting. ⟦q25.1|26+27+28+29⟧ For as there is no principle in nature of what is above nature, so neither of what is contrary to nature and in revolt. ⟦q25.1|30⟧ Hence the teacher did not declare that it does not follow in every way and wholly, but tempered it, saying: «not by all means», and «for the most part»; to which is understood "sometimes, and seldom", because of the difficulty the many have in being led up to virtue.

⟦q25.1|31⟧ For the willing of the human [element] in the Saviour, even if it was natural, was yet not bare as ours is, just as neither was the human itself, being deified above us to the uttermost by the union, upon which sinlessness also properly depends. ⟦q25.1|32⟧ But ours is manifestly bare and in no way sinless, because of the swerving that happens this way and that: a swerving that does not alter nature, but turns aside movement, or rather, to say it more truly, exchanges its mode. ⟦q25.1|33⟧ And this is plain from our doing many things irrationally, and yet in no way passing over into an irrational essence from the innate rational [essence] that is in us.

⟦q25.1|33⟧ [0237] So the human [element] in us is not one thing and that in the Saviour another; nor is the willing other, according to the principle of nature at least, even if [it is] otherwise [in him], above us; for in a divine way he subsisted, and it was shaped, through the utmost union with the divine. ⟦q25.1|34⟧ But again, to fit, quite suitably indeed, each of the things in us that are blamed—such as opposition, or opposed array, and whatever belongs to this list—even if, separating the natures inseparably in conception, they go on to the next nature: yet to take the manhood of Christ along with them in these is not lawful. ⟦q25.1|35+36⟧ For if not even in us are they considered as suitable [to nature], but as contrary to nature and reason, how can one attach them to that [manhood] even by conception or by after-conception, so to speak? ⟦q25.1|35+36⟧ But only by appropriation, out of pity, as to the head of the whole body, just as to a physician [are attributed] the passions of the sick man, until God, who became man for our sake, frees us from them; yet consuming and wiping them out of us completely by the power of his embodiment.

⟦q25.1|37⟧ For the account of passions is twofold: one of penalty, the other of dishonour; and the one characterizes our nature, the other counterfeits it throughout. ⟦q25.1|38⟧ The former, then, he accepted essentially, willing as man for our sake, at once confirming the nature and loosing the condemnation against us; the latter, again, as lover of man, he appropriated by way of economy, it being known in us and in our insubordinate mode, so that, as fire wax, or as the sun the vapour of the earth, consuming it utterly out of us, he might make the communication of his own things, and prepare us to be dispassionate from now, and incorruptible according to the promise. ⟦q25.1|39⟧ We must, then, welcome, as is fitting, above all the zeal and at the same time the labour of him who has been earnest in such things for piety's sake, but urge him toward a more exact diligence with the words of the Fathers, because of the abusers of good things, who out of excessive folly do not concede even that which they could not destroy even if they wished.

⟦q25.1|40⟧ As for Honorius, the pope of the [Church] of the Romans, I do not think that in the letter written to Sergius he speaks against the duality of the innate wills in Christ by saying *one will*; rather he speaks for it and, as is likely, establishes it, saying this not to the setting-aside of the human and natural will of the Saviour, but [meaning] that no will of the flesh or passionate thought in any way went before his seedless conception and incorrupt birth. ⟦q25.1|41⟧ For only the divine and paternal volition, through the only-begotten Son, who himself worked his own incarnation, and by the concurrence of the Holy Spirit, wrought it. ⟦q25.1|42⟧ And that he holds to this meaning is plain from this. ⟦q25.1|43⟧ For having said that because of the ineffable

⟦q26.1|1⟧ [0240] union of the human and divine nature, both God is said to have suffered, and manhood to have come down from heaven with the Godhead, and having in this way shown the utmost exchange, by interchange, of the things that belong naturally to each nature of the one Christ and Son, he adds, saying: «Whence also we confess one will of our Lord Jesus Christ.» ⟦q26.1|2⟧ How, he says? ⟦q26.1|3⟧ «Since manifestly our nature, not sin, was assumed by the Godhead»—that is, not from sin; all but speaking in unison with the great Athanasius, who writes these things against the impious Apollinarius: «He was born of a woman, raising up for himself the form of man from the first moulding, in a display of flesh without carnal wills and human thoughts, in an image of newness; for the volition is of the Godhead only, since the whole nature also is of the Godhead.» [n: Galat. iv, 4.]

⟦q26.1|4⟧ For since the procession of the Word's birth according to the flesh for our sake came about above us—for no passionate volition or thought of flesh went before it, as is seen in our case, because of the pleasure that has prevailed over the race since the deceit, but the volition of the Godhead only, through the Son, who, as I said, himself worked his own embodiment by the good pleasure of the Father and the co-operation of the all-holy Spirit, making new in himself and through himself the mode of birth brought in upon the nature, and making his own conception without seed from the holy Theotokos and Ever-virgin Mary—considering this ineffable principle of his birth, the one said «only the volition of the Godhead in him», the other «one will of the Lord Jesus Christ, since manifestly, he says, our nature, and not sin, was assumed by the Godhead: without carnal wills and human thoughts», as the divine Athanasius says; not, however, [meaning] that he did not also have, as man, together with being God by nature, a human and natural will, just as also [he had] the divine and paternal.
